(suppliedMaxPlaintextBytes < 1\n                || suppliedMaxPlaintextBytes > MAX_ALLOWED_PLAINTEXT_BYTES) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Plaintext size limit is outside the allowed range\");\n        }\n        if (suppliedKeys == null || suppliedKeys.isEmpty() || suppliedKeys.size() > MAX_KEYS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Key ring size is outside the allowed range\");\n        }\n\n        Map<String, KeyEntry> copied = new LinkedHashMap<>();\n        SecureRandom createdRandom = null;\n        byte[] createdUnknownKey = null;\n        try {\n            for (Map.Entry<String, byte[]> supplied : suppliedKeys.entrySet()) {\n                if (copied.size() >= MAX_KEYS) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Key ring size is outside the allowed range\");\n                }\n                if (supplied == null) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Key ring entries must not be null\");\n                }\n                String id = supplied.getKey();\n                validateIdentifier(id, \"key identifier\");\n                byte[] key = supplied.getValue();\n                if (key == null || key.length != AES_KEY_BYTES) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Every key must be exactly 256 bits\");\n                }\n                if (copied.containsKey(id)) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Key identifiers must be unique\");\n                }\n                for (KeyEntry existing : copied.values()) {\n                    if (MessageDigest.isEqual(existing.bytes, key)) {\n                        throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                                \"Each key identifier must have distinct key material\");\n                    }\n                }\n                copied.put(id, new KeyEntry(key.clone()));\n            }\n            if (!copied.containsKey(suppliedActiveKeyId)) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Active key must exist in the key ring\");\n            }\n            // Initialize fallible provider state while copied keys are still under\n            // this constructor's failure-cleanup guard.\n            createdRandom = new SecureRandom();\n            createdUnknownKey = new byte[AES_KEY_BYTES];\n            createdRandom.nextBytes(createdUnknownKey);\n        } catch (RuntimeException failure) {\n            copied.values().forEach(KeyEntry::destroy);\n            if (createdUnknownKey != null) {\n                Arrays.fill(createdUnknownKey, (byte) 0);\n            }\n            throw failure;\n        }\n\n        this.keys = Collections.unmodifiableMap(copied);\n        this.activeKeyId = suppliedActiveKeyId;\n        this.purpose = suppliedPurpose.getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);\n        this.maxPlaintextBytes = suppliedMaxPlaintextBytes;\n        this.random = createdRandom;\n        this.unknownKey = createdUnknownKey;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Creates a protector by defensively copying raw AES-256 keys.\n     *\n     * @param keys trusted server-side key IDs mapped to 32-byte random AES keys\n     * @param activeKeyId trusted server-side ID used only for new encryption\n     * @param purpose stable, non-secret domain separator for this data type\n     * @param maxPlaintextBytes accepted size limit from 1 byte through 16 MiB\n     */\n    public static AesGcmDataProtector create(\n            Map<String, byte[]> keys,\n            String activeKeyId,\n            String purpose,\n            int maxPlaintextBytes) {\n        return new AesGcmDataProtector(keys, activeKeyId, purpose, maxPlaintextBytes);\n    }\n\n    /** Generates a new runtime AES-256 key. The caller owns and must erase it. */\n    public static byte[] generateKey() {\n        byte[] key = new byte[AES_KEY_BYTES];\n        new SecureRandom().nextBytes(key);\n        return key;\n    }\n\n    /** Encrypts and authenticates plaintext plus its caller-supplied context. */\n    public byte[] encrypt(byte[] plaintext, byte[] context) throws DataProtectionException {\n  DestroyableAesKey operationKey = new DestroyableAesKey(entry.copyBytes());\n            try {\n                Cipher cipher = Cipher.getInstance(CIPHER);\n                cipher.init(Cipher.ENCRYPT_MODE, operationKey,\n                        new GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, nonce));\n                addAuthenticatedData(cipher, header, context);\n                byte[] sealed = cipher.doFinal(plaintext);\n                try {\n                    byte[] envelope = new byte[header.length + sealed.length];\n                    System.arraycopy(header, 0, envelope, 0, header.length);\n                    System.arraycopy(sealed, 0, envelope, header.length, sealed.length);\n                    return envelope;\n                } finally {\n                    Arrays.fill(sealed, (byte) 0);\n                }\n            } catch (GeneralSecurityException | RuntimeException failure) {\n                throw new DataProtectionException();\n            } }\n\n    /**\n     * Authenticates and decrypts an envelope. Every attacker-visible failure has\n     * the same exception type and message.\n     */\n    public byte[] decrypt(byte[] envelope, byte[] context) throws DataProtectionException {\n        validateContext(context);\n        if (envelope == null\n                || envelope.length < FIXED_HEADER_BYTES + 1 + TAG_BYTES\n                || envelope.length > FIXED_HEADER_BYTES +         byte[] operationBytes = knownKey ? knownEntry.copyBytes() : unknownKey.clone();\n            DestroyableAesKey operationKey = new DestroyableAesKey(operationBytes);\n            byte[] plaintext = null;\n            try {\n                Cipher cipher = Cipher.getInstance(CIPHER);\n                cipher.init(Cipher.DECRYPT_MODE, operationKey,\n                        new GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, parsed.nonce));\n                addAuthenticatedData(cipher, parsed.header, context);\n                plaintext = cipher.doFinal(envelope, parsed.header.length,\n                        envelope.length - parsed.header.length);\n                if (!knownKey || plaintext.length > maxPlaintextBytes) {\n                    Arrays.fill(plaintext, (byte) 0);\n                    throw new DataProtectionException();\n                }\n                return plaintext;\n            } catch (DataProtectionException failure) {\n                throw failure;\n            } new DataProtectionException();\n            } finally {\n                operationKey.destroy();\n                parsed.destroy();\n            }\n        } finally {\n     Arrays.fill(purpose, (byte) 0);\n                closed = true;\n            }\n        } finally {\n            lifecycleLock.writeLock().unlock();\n        }\n    }\n\n    private ParsedEnvelope Authenticated data protection component\n\nThis is a small Java 17 library for encrypting application data. It has no CLI,\nUI, administration endpoints, persistence layer, logging framework, or external\ndependencies. It uses the JDK's `AES/GCM/NoPadding` implementation with a\n256-bit key, a new 96-bit `SecureRandom` nonce for every encryption, and a full\n128-bit authentication tag. These choices follow [NIST SP 800-38D](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/38/d/final)\nand the [Java Cryptography Architecture guidance](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/17/security/java-cryptography-architecture-jca-reference-guide.html).\n\n## Use\n\nKeys must come from a server-side secret manager, KMS export, or equivalent\nprotected runtime configuration—not source control, command-line arguments, or\nclient input. The example generates a runtime key only to show the API:\n\n```java\nbyte[] key = AesGcmDataProtector.generateKey();\nbyte[] plaintext = loadSensitiveBytes();\nbyte[] context = \"tenant/123/customer/456\".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n\ntry (AesGcmDataProtector protector = AesGcmDataProtector.create(\n        Map.of(\"key-2026-10\", key),\n        \"key-2026-10\",\n        \"customer-profile\",\n        64 * 1024)) {\n    Arrays.fill(key, (byte) 0); // the component already holds its private copy\n    byte[] envelope = protector.encrypt(plaintext, context);\n    Arrays.fill(plaintext, (byte) 0);\n\n    byte[] recovered = protector.decrypt(envelope, context);\n    try {\n        consume(recovered);\n    } finally {\n        Arrays.fill(recovered, (byte) 0);\n    }\n}\n```\n\nThe non-secret `purpose` is a stable domain separator. `context` should identify\nthe intended tenant, record, and field so a valid ciphertext cannot be moved to\nanother location. Exactly the same bytes are required to decrypt. The caller\nmust authorize access to that tenant/record before invoking this component.\n\nFor rotation, configure the old and new keys together and make the new key ID\nactive. Encryption always uses the server-configured active key. Decryption may\nuse an authenticated envelope key ID only after matching it against the trusted\nkey ring. Retire an old key only after all of its ciphertext has been migrated.\nNever reuse the same key bytes under multiple IDs. Track total encryptions across\nall processes and deployments and rotate before 2^32 invocations under one key;\nthe component also enforces that limit atomically within each instance.\n\nCompile and run the dependency-free tests:\n\n```text\nmkdir -p out\njavac -d out $(rg --files src/main/java src/test/java -g '*.java')\njava -cp out com.example.security.crypto.AesGcmDataProtectorTest\n```\n\n`out` is build output and must not be deployed as writable shared code.\n\n## Trust boundaries and adversarial review\n\nEvery input is treated as hostile:\n\n| Input | Source | Validation/use |\n|---|---|---|\n| Raw keys | secret manager, KMS export, configuration, database, or runtime caller | Non-null, exactly 32 bytes, at most 32 distinct keys; defensively copied; duplicate material is rejected. Never parsed from strings by this library. |\n| Key IDs and active ID | configuration; envelope ID is client-controlled | Strict 1–64 character ASCII allowlist. The active ID must exist in trusted server-side state. An envelope ID only selects a configured key and is covered by the GCM tag. |\n| Purpose | configuration | Strict 1–64 character ASCII allowlist and authenticated as a domain separator. |\n| Plaintext | database, file, service, or client | Non-null and bounded by the validated configured limit (1 byte–16 MiB). Empty plaintext is allowed. |\n| Context | database, configuration, service, or client | Non-null, 0–64 KiB, length-delimited, and authenticated but not encrypted. It never grants authorization. |\n| Ciphertext envelope | database, file, service, or client | Bounded before parsing; exact magic, version, algorithm, ID length/characters, nonce, and minimum tag length are checked. Header, context, and purpose are authenticated before plaintext is returned. Unknown keys perform a dummy authenticated-decryption attempt and fail generically. |\n| Size limit | configuration | Integer restricted to 1 byte–16 MiB. Invalid values are rejected; no permissive default exists. |\n\nAdversarial cases considered include tampering, truncation, algorithm/version\ndowngrade, unknown-key probing, cross-purpose and cross-record substitution,\nnonce reuse, oversized-input denial of service, mutable caller arrays, key\nrotation mistakes, concurrent close/use, serialization, and exception leakage.\n\n## How the requested security rules apply\n\n1. **Trust boundaries.** The table above enumerates every input. All are type,\n   length, range, and/or allowlist checked. Ciphertext-controlled metadata is\n   authenticated and can only reference trusted server-side keys.\n2. **Secrets.** There are no embedded keys or credentials. Keys enter as runtime\n   byte arrays, are never included in messages or `toString`, cannot be\n   serialized by the operation-scoped key class, and are never logged or written\n   to disk. Do not put keys in environment strings or command-line arguments;\n   use a secret manager with least-privilege file/socket permissions if needed.\n3. **Authentication and access.** This component neither authenticates users nor\n   makes authorization decisions. Its caller must check server-side access before\n   use. Account/source rate limiting is therefore not applicable; cryptographic\n   failures are not login attempts and rate limiting here could enable lockout.\n4. **Identifiers and tokens.** No session IDs, cookies, or authentication tokens\n   exist. Nonces are generated internally with `SecureRandom` and cannot be\n   supplied by a caller. Externally stored record identifiers belong in\n   authenticated `context`; remaining authorization risk stays with the caller.\n5. **Sensitive data.** Inputs and outputs are bounded. Internal raw-key copies\n   are erased on close and operation copies are erased immediately. Callers must\n   erase their own key/plaintext arrays and, if persisting envelopes, create files\n   with owner-only permissions. The JVM/provider may retain unavoidable internal\n   copies until garbage collection, so use a KMS/HSM design when that threat is\n   in scope.\n6. **Failure behavior.** Runtime operations expose only\n   `DataProtectionException` with one fixed message, no cause, and no stack trace.\n   Invalid input is rejected with no fallback. The library deliberately emits no\n   logs; applications may record a generic failure metric but must not log keys,\n   plaintext, context, envelopes, exception inputs, or existence-sensitive data.\n7. **Concurrency.** The component is thread-safe. Per-key usage reservation is\n   atomic. A read/write lifecycle lock makes use-versus-close atomic, so key\n   erasure waits for active operations and new operations fail closed.\n8. **Prefer the platform.** Cryptography and randomness use only Java 17 JCA/JCE\n   primitives. No cipher, tag, random generator, or encoding is hand-rolled, and\n   no third-party or remotely downloaded code is required.\n\nJava cannot guarantee erasure of provider/JIT/GC-managed copies. Closing the\ncomponent and clearing caller-owned arrays is defense in depth, not a substitute\nfor process isolation, heap-dump controls, least privilege, and a managed KMS or\nHSM for environments requiring non-exportable keys.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"ab1883b5-c184-44d9-a79d-4b8b31ace157","session_id":"4fcaa958-b696-4c8e-ae33-3f9a2fe1776c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"ab1883b5-c184-44d9-a79d-4b8b31ace157","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now let me get precise line numbers. let me analyze the code carefully. This looks like a deliberately-hardened \"secure\" crypto component. The task is a security review — I need to find real vulnerabilities. Let me think carefully about the actual security properties.\n\nKey areas:\n\n### 1. Nonce/IV handling and GCM IV limit\n`MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY = 0xffff_ffffL` = 2^32 - 1. NIST SP 800-38D limit for random 96-bit IVs under one key is 2^32 invocations. The comment says \"NIST's limit for independently generated random GCM IVs under one key\". The counter is per-instance (`KeyEntry.encryptionCount` is per AesGcmDataProtector instance), and the README admits \"Track total encryptions across all processes and deployments\". Within one instance the limit is 2^32-1 which is at/below the NIST limit. Actually NIST says re-key when number of invocations of GCM with same (K,H) > 2^32. Setting the cap at 2^32-1 within an instance is fine — but across instances the counter resets. This is a documented limitation. Is it a real finding? Multi-instance nonce collision risk: with random 96-bit nonces, birthday-bound collision probability. At 2^32 random nonces, collision probability ≈ (2^32)^2 / 2^129 = 2^64/2^129 = 2^-65. Very small. Across many processes each doing 2^32... it would take ~2^48 encryptions for meaningful collision risk (birthday bound at 2^47.5 for 96-bit nonce). So the 2^32 cap is much more conservative than the birthday bound. Not a real vulnerability. The README covers it.\n\nHmm — but is the per-instance counter a genuine control? It resets on restart. This is at most a Low/informational note.\n\n### 2. `equals()` in DestroyableAesKey — the `hashCode()` bug\n`hashCode()` returns `Arrays.hashCode(bytes)` where bytes is the raw key. This leaks... well, it's a hash of the key, computed from key material. That's a side channel: `Arrays.hashCode` is not constant-time, so timing could leak key info? Only if an attacker can observe hashCode timing/comparisons — not realistic here. Also `hashCode()` is a deterministic function of the key material, so if the object were placed in a HashSet and the hash logged... marginal. Real issue: `hashCode()` violates the \"destroy → hash of 0\" but returns 0 when destroyed, so hashCode changes after destroy (contract violation, not security). Minor.\n\nBut bigger: `equals(Object other)` — it calls `getEncoded()` on `other`, i.e. on an arbitrary `SecretKey`. This is only reachable if equals is invoked. `equals` is not called anywhere within the class. Is this a vulnerability? An attacker-provided SecretKey... no, the other object would need to be attacker-controlled and equals called on our key. Not exploitable. Purpose length is fixed at construction (per instance) and header is self-delimiting (magic+ver+alg+keyIdLen+keyId+nonce). So the concatenation is unambiguous — domain separation across the header boundary is fine. Actually the header is also transmitted in the envelope and covered by AAD. Fine.\n\nIs there a cross-purpose attack? `purpose` is a per-instance config; two different purposes produce different AAD → cross-purpose substitution fails. NIST SP 800-38D §8.3: for the random IV construction, ensure the probability of duplicate (H, V) pairs is negligible; the commonly cited bound is 2^32 invocations for 96-bit random IVs. Actually, let me reconsider something more concrete about the nonce budget: `MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY = 0xffff_ffffL` = 4,294,967,295 ≈ 2^32-1. The comment claims it's NIST's limit for random IVs. SP 800-38D says for random IVs: \"the number of invocations of GCM for a given content key shall be limited to less than 2^32\". The code enforces ≤ 2^32-1 per instance — so a single instance never exceeds. Across J instances the total can be J×(2^32-1). For random 96-bit IVs the real constraint is collision probability: with n encryptions under one key, P(collision) ≈ n²/2^97. At n = 2^40, P ≈ 2^-17 (6.9e-6) — already arguably too high for high-value data! At n = 2^48 total, P ≈ 0.5. So a single key used across 1024 instances × 2^30 encryptions each = 2^40 total → collision chance ~1 in 145k, and each collision is a full loss of confidentiality + authenticity for those two messages. So the aggregate-tracking caveat is *operationally* significant: the code's enforcement is instance-local while the security property is per-key. That's a legitimate Low-to-Medium finding. SKILL.md\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.DS-01\n- PR.DS-02\n- PR.DS-10\nmitre_attack:\n- T1600\n- T1573\n- T1553\n- T1486\n---\n# Implementing AES Encryption for Data at Rest\n\n## Overview\n\nAES (Advanced Encryption Standard) is a symmetric block cipher standardized by NIST (FIPS 197) used to protect classified and sensitive data. This skill covers implementing AES-256 encryption in GCM mode for encrypting files and data stores at rest, including proper key derivation, IV/nonce management, and authenticated encryption.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When deploying or configuring implementing aes encryption for data at rest capabilities in your environment\n- When establishing security controls aligned to compliance requirements\n- When building or improving security architecture for this domain\n- When conducting security assessments that require this implementation\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Implement AES-256-GCM encryption and decryption for files\n- Derive encryption keys from passwords using PBKDF2 and Argon2\n- Manage initialization vectors (IVs) and nonces securely\n- Encrypt and decrypt entire directory trees\n- Implement authenticated encryption to detect tampering\n- Handle large files with streaming encryption\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### AES Modes of Operation\n\n| Mode | Authentication | Parallelizable | Use Case |\n|------|---------------|----------------|----------|\n| GCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | Yes            | Network data, file encryption |\n| CBC  | No            | Decrypt only   | Legacy systems, disk encryption |\n| CTR  | No            | Yes            | Streaming encryption |\n| CCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | No             | IoT, constrained environments |\n\n### Key Derivation\n\nNever use raw passwords as encryption keys. Always derive keys using:\n- **PBKDF2**: NIST-approved, widely supported (minimum 600,000 iterations as of 2024)\n- **Argon2id**: Winner of Password Hashing Competition, memory-hard\n- **scrypt**: Memory-hard, good alternative to Argon2\n\n### Nonce/IV Management\n\n- GCM requires a 96-bit (12-byte) nonce that must NEVER be reused with the same key\n- Generate nonces using `os.urandom()` (CSPRNG)\n- Store nonce alongside ciphertext (it is not secret)\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Install the `cryptography` library: `pip install cryptography`\n2. Generate or derive an encryption key\n3. Create a random nonce for each encryption operation\n4. Encrypt data using AES-256-GCM with the key and nonce\n5. Store nonce + ciphertext + authentication tag together\n6. For decryption, extract nonce, verify tag, and decrypt\n\n## Encrypted File Format\n\n```\n[salt: 16 bytes][nonce: 12 bytes][ciphertext: variable][tag: 16 bytes]\n```\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Always use authenticated encryption (GCM, CCM) to prevent tampering\n- Never reuse a nonce with the same key (catastrophic in GCM)\n- Use at least 256-bit keys for long-term data protection\n- Securely wipe keys from memory after use when possible\n- Rotate encryption keys periodically per organizational policy\n- For disk-level encryption, consider XTS mode (AES-XTS)\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] AES-256-GCM encryption produces valid ciphertext\n- [ ] Decryption recovers original plaintext exactly\n- [ ] Authentication tag detects any ciphertext modification\n- [ ] Key derivation uses sufficient iterations/parameters\n- [ ] Nonces are never reused for the same key\n- [ ] Large files (>1GB) can be processed via streaming\n- [ ] Encrypted file format includes all necessary metadata"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"56c0fdd5-363b-4223-ad14-86280aea5b95","session_id":"4fcaa958-b696-4c8e-ae33-3f9a2fe1776c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"56c0fdd5-363b-4223-ad14-86280aea5b95","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Applied skill 1 checks against the code:\n- AES-256 ✓ (line 34 AES_KEY_BYTES=32, \"AES/GCM/NoPadding\" line 33)\n- GCM AEAD ✓, 96-bit nonce ✓ (line 36), fresh per encrypt (line 162-163), CSPRNG ✓ (SecureRandom)\n- Nonce never reused with same key → **enforced only per instance** (line 46 + KeyEntry.reserveEncryption 342-351). id: F1006.001\n    name: 'Account Takeover: Exposed API Key'\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n  - id: F1004\n    name: Access with Stolen Session Cookie\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n---\n# Performing Cryptographic Audit of Application\n\n## Overview\n\nA cryptographic audit systematically reviews an application's use of cryptographic primitives, protocols, and key management to identify vulnerabilities such as weak algorithms, insecure modes, hardcoded keys, insufficient entropy, and protocol misconfigurations. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 Let me compute: for random 96-bit nonces, collision probability after n draws ≈ n²/2^97. At n = 2^32: 2^64/2^97 = 2^-33 ≈ 1.2e-10. That's the accumulated birthday probability — small but this is exactly NIST's bound rationale. Using the full budget with no margin means any miscounting (per-instance, restart resets counter to 0!) silently consumes the margin.\n\nKey point: the counter resets to zero on every process restart! `encryptionCount` is an instance field starting at 0. Executive Summary\n\nThe component under review is a small, self-contained envelope-encryption helper: it seals\nbyte payloads with AES-256-GCM using a per-call random 96-bit IV, prefixes a versioned header\n(`MAGIC` + version + algorithm + key-ID length + key ID + nonce), binds a caller-supplied\n`purpose` string and per-call `context` as additional authenticated data, and supports a small\nnamed key ring with an active key for encryption and any known key for decryption.\n\nThe cryptographic core is sound and, in most respects, better than typical generated code:\nauthenticated encryption with a 128-bit tag, a fresh `SecureRandom` nonce per call,\nlength-delimited AAD framing that prevents purpose/context splicing, strict envelope length and\nformat validation *before* parsing, uniform generic failure for every rejection path (no\npadding-style or key-ID enumeration oracle), dummy decryption under a random key for unknown key\nIDs, defensive key copying with erasure, rejection of duplicate key material, blocked\nserialization of key objects, and fail-closed lifecycle locking after `close()`.\n\nThree issues remain. The one that matters is the nonce-reuse safety valve: the code caps\nencryptions per key at NIST's full `2^32 - 1` random-IV budget and enforces it with a counter\nthat lives inside a single JVM instance and starts at zero on every construction. Any deployment\nwith more than one replica, or with ephemeral/restarting processes, sharing a key ID can therefore\ncollectively draw far more random 96-bit nonces under that key than the bound allows, and nothing\nin the process notices. A GCM nonce repeat under one key is not a graceful failure — it loses\nconfidentiality for the colliding messages and enables forgery. The project's own `README.md`\nstates the limit is enforced \"within each instance\" and asks operators to track usage across all\nprocesses, which is the correct caveat but means the in-code control is a no-op in exactly the\ndeployments where it is needed; setting the ceiling at the absolute maximum leaves no margin to\nabsorb that gap. The other two findings are latent defects in the private `DestroyableAesKey`\nhelper (`equals()` destroys key material it does not own and throws instead of returning `false`;\n`hashCode()` derives a fingerprint from live key bytes, defeating the class's own redaction).\nNeither is reachable from today's call sites — the class is private and its instances only ever\nreach `Cipher.init` — so they are reported as low-severity hardening items rather than exploitable\ndefects. No hardcoded secrets, weak algorithms, unsafe deserialization sinks, injection sinks,\nlogging of sensitive material, or non-cryptographic RNG use were found anywhere in the source.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java` | Envelope encryptor/decryptor: versioned header, named key ring, active-key selection, AES-256-GCM sealing with `purpose` + `context` as AAD | Random 96-bit IV per call from `SecureRandom`; 128-bit tag; per-key encryption budget gate (`reserveEncryption()`); pre-parse envelope length/format bounds; constant-time magic compare and duplicate-key compare; dummy decryption under random `unknownKey` for unknown key IDs; generic failure for all rejections; key copies with erasure (`DestroyableAesKey.destroy()`); read/write-lock lifecycle with `closed` flag; plaintext/context/key-ring size caps; `writeObject` blocked on key class |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/DataProtectionException.java` | Single uniform checked exception for every crypto failure | `super(GENERIC_MESSAGE, null, false, false)` — no cause, no suppression, no stack trace, so failures cannot leak which check tripped; `serialVersionUID` fixed, no `readObject` |\n| `src/test/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtectorTest.java` | `main()`-driven self-test harness | Asserts nonce freshness across calls, tamper/context-mismatch rejection, rotation + unknown-key rejection, fail-closed bounds/lifecycle, invalid-config rejection, and that the thrown message is the generic one with `getCause() == null` |\n| `README.md` | Usage and trust-boundary documentation | Documents 96-bit `SecureRandom` nonce, 128-bit tag, keys sourced from a secret manager/KMS, and explicitly states the 2^32 encryption ceiling is enforced \"within each instance\" only |\n\nReviewed in place from the read-only mount. Nothing in that folder was modified; nothing was\nexecuted, built, installed, or imported — analysis is static only.\n\n## 3. Skills Applied\n\nLibrary skills whose instructions were loaded and used to drive the audit:\n\n- `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application` — provided the weakness-category checklist\n  (primitive/mode choice, IV/nonce handling, key management and lifecycle, storage of key\n  material, side-channel and oracle exposure, serialization of crypto objects) used to structure\n  the pass over `AesGcmDataProtector.java`.\n- `implementing-aes-encryption-for-data-at-rest` — supplied the GCM rules the findings are judged\n  against: never reuse a (key, nonce) pair, 96-bit random IVs, 128-bit tags, the bounded number of\n  encryptions permitted per key under random IVs, and authentication of associated data. This is\n  the skill that identifies SEC-01.\n- `testing-for-sensitive-data-exposure` — guided the sweep for key/secret material reaching\n  `toString()`, exception text, logs, hashes, or serialization streams, and for information leaked\n  through error differentiation (SEC-03 and the section 6 checks on the generic exception and\n  redacted `toString()`).\n- `exploiting-insecure-deserialization` — used to test the Java-serialization markers that are\n  present (`serialVersionUID` on both the exception and `DestroyableAesKey`, a\n  `private void writeObject`) against the absence of any deserialization sink.\n\nConsulted by description only, then not applied (the code manages keys through a caller-supplied\nring and does not itself integrate a vault or a KMS): `implementing-secrets-management-with-vault`,\n`implementing-envelope-encryption-with-aws-kms`.\n\n## 4. Summary of Findings\n\n| ID | Title | Severity | CWE | Location |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| SEC-01 | Per-key random-nonce budget set to the full NIST maximum and counted per instance | Medium | CWE-323 | `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:45-46` |\n| SEC-02 | `DestroyableAesKey.equals()` zeroes key material it does not own and throws on destroyed keys | Low | CWE-664 | `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:420-437` |\n| SEC-03 | `hashCode()` derives a 32-bit fingerprint from live AES key bytes | Low | CWE-200 | `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:440-445` |\n\n## 5. Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Per-key random-nonce budget set to the full NIST maximum and counted per instance\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-323: Reusing a Nonce, Key Pair in Encryption\n- **Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:45-46`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n    // NIST's limit for independently generated random GCM IVs under one key.\n    private static final long MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY = 0xffff_ffffL;\n```\n\nThe gate that consumes this budget is per-instance state\n(`src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:334-350`):\n\n```java\n    private static final class KeyEntry {\n        private final byte[] bytes;\n        private final AtomicLong encryptionCount = new AtomicLong();\n```\n\n```java\n        private boolean reserveEncryption() {\n            long current;\n            do {\n                current = encryptionCount.get();\n                if (current >= MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY) {\n                    return false;\n                }\n            } while (!encryptionCount.compareAndSet(current, current + 1));\n            return true;\n        }\n```\n\nand is reached from `encrypt()` on every sealing call\n(`src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:159-163`):\n\n```java\n            KeyEntry entry = keys.get(activeKeyId);\n            if (!entry.reserveEncryption()) {\n                throw new DataProtectionException();\n            }\n\n            byte[] nonce = new byte[NONCE_BYTES];\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\n`encrypt()` draws each nonce at random (`random.nextBytes(nonce)`), so the safety of the whole\nscheme rests on never repeating a (key, nonce) pair. NIST SP 800-38D caps independent random IVs\nat `2^32` invocations per key precisely because the collision probability for random 96-bit nonces\nbecomes uncomfortable beyond that point. This code adopts the ceiling verbatim *and* enforces it\nwith a counter that is reset to zero every time the object is constructed. The consequence is that\nthe ceiling measures per-instance, per-lifetime usage, while the real budget is per-key across\nevery process that holds that key:\n\n- N replicas that share the active key ID each get their own fresh `2^32 - 1` allowance, so the\n  fleet's true allowance is N × 2^32 — the bound is exceeded before any single instance trips its\n  limit, and no instance can observe the others.\n- Autoscaled or containerised services that restart constantly never approach the counter limit at\n  all: the counter resets on each boot while the *key* keeps accumulating nonces across boots. In\n  that very common topology the check effectively never fires, no matter how much the key is used.\n- Setting the ceiling at the absolute maximum leaves zero margin for exactly this undercounting.\n  There is no \"rotate soon\" band below the hard stop; the counter can only ever fire at the last\n  permitted nonce, by which time the accumulated birthday probability for a fleet-wide\n  undercount is already the risk being accepted.\n\nThe failure mode when a nonce does repeat under one key is not graceful: two envelopes sharing a\nnonce under the same key leak the XOR of their plaintexts and, because the GHASH subkeys are\nidentical, expose the authentication key, which allows existential forgery of new ciphertexts that\ndecrypt successfully. That is a confidentiality *and* integrity break in a component whose entire\njob is to provide both, which is why this is rated Medium despite requiring very heavy key use to\ntrigger. The project's own `README.md` acknowledges the gap — \"the component also enforces that\nlimit atomically **within each instance**\" and asks the operator to \"track total encryptions across\nall processes and deployments\" — which is honest documentation, but a documented caveat is not an\nenforced control. A safety valve that cannot fire in the deployments where the bound is actually\napproached should be set low enough that the limit is hit *because* of the undercount, not\ndespite it.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nTreat the per-instance counter as a lower bound on true key usage and size the ceiling so the\nundercount from restarts and replicas is absorbed — rotate long before the mathematical limit, and\nmake the exhaustion path explicit so callers can rotate instead of silently hitting a wall:\n\n```java\n    // Random 96-bit nonces must never repeat under one key. This counter is per\n    // instance, so the ceiling has to absorb every restart and every replica\n    // that shares the key id: use a conservative fraction of NIST's 2^32 bound\n    // and rotate well before the mathematical limit.\n    private static final long MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY = 1L << 28;\n```\n\n```java\n        private boolean reserveEncryption() {\n            long current;\n            do {\n                current = encryptionCount.get();\n                if (current >= MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY) {\n                    return false;\n                }\n            } while (!encryptionCount.compareAndSet(current, current + 1));\n            return true;\n        }\n```\n\nand surface the exhaustion as a rotation signal rather than an opaque crypto failure, so that\nreaching the (now conservative) ceiling drives a key-ring change instead of an outage that\ntempting to work around by raising the constant:\n\n```java\n            KeyEntry entry = keys.get(activeKeyId);\n            if (!entry.reserveEncryption()) {\n                throw new DataProtectionException(\n                        \"Active key has reached its encryption budget and must be rotated\");\n            }\n\n            byte[] nonce = new byte[NONCE_BYTES];\n```\n\nTwo complementary hardening measures are worth taking with this change: (a) back the budget with\nusage that survives restarts (an external counter, or key-usage telemetry from the KMS/secret\nmanager) so the fleet-wide truth is visible and rotation can be triggered by real consumption\nrather than by the local lower bound; (b) if the deployment cannot guarantee per-key usage bounds,\nswitch from random 96-bit IVs to the deterministic per-key invocation-counter construction of\nSP 800-38D §8.2.2, which turns nonce uniqueness into a monotonic counter obligation instead of a\nbirthday-bound one — in which case the counter must be persisted, not in-memory.\n\n### SEC-02: `DestroyableAesKey.equals()` zeroes key material it does not own and throws on destroyed keys\n\n- **Severity:** Low\n- **CWE:** CWE-664: Improper Control of a Resource Through its Lifetime\n- **Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:420-437`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n        @Override\n        public boolean equals(Object other) {\n            if (this == other) {\n                return true;\n            }\n            if (!(other instanceof SecretKey)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n            byte[] mine = getEncoded();\n            byte[] theirs = ((SecretKey) other).getEncoded();\n            try {\n                return theirs != null && MessageDigest.isEqual(mine, theirs);\n            } finally {\n                Arrays.fill(mine, (byte) 0);\n                if (theirs != null) {\n                    Arrays.fill(theirs, (byte) 0);\n                }\n            }\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\n`equals()` is expected to be a side-effect-free predicate. This implementation mutates two buffers\nthat are not its own, and both mutations are unsafe assumptions about someone else's contract:\n\n- `theirs` is whatever the *other* `SecretKey` implementation's `getEncoded()` chose to hand back.\n  For `DestroyableAesKey` and for `SecretKeySpec` that happens to be a clone, so the fill is\n  harmless. But `SecretKey` does not require a copy: an implementation may return its internal\n  array (custom providers, HSM/PKCS#11 stubs, and most non-JDK key wrappers do exactly what is\n  convenient for them). For such a key, `a.equals(b)` silently overwrites `b`'s live secret with\n  zeros. `b`'s owner never asked for that and cannot detect it — `isDestroyed()` on that object\n  still reports false while `getEncoded()` now returns 32 zero bytes, so the next cipher\n  initialised with `b` runs under an all-zero AES key and produces ciphertext that looks perfectly\n  valid. Destroying a resource outside your own lifetime control is the defect here; the erasure\n  discipline that is correct for `mine` does not transfer to a buffer you do not own.\n- The constructor is invoked on `this` via the `synchronized getEncoded()`, which throws\n  `IllegalStateException(\"Key is unavailable\")` once the key has been destroyed. A destroyed key\n  therefore makes `equals()` throw rather than return `false`, violating the `Object.equals`\n  contract (\"implementation must be consistent ... must not throw an exception\") and turning an\n  ordinary comparison into an unexpected failure.\n\nBoth are latent rather than reachable today. `DestroyableAesKey` is a `private static final` nested\nclass; its only instances are created inside `encrypt()`/`decrypt()` and passed straight to\n`Cipher.init`, and nothing in this source tree calls `.equals()` on it or uses it as a\nhash-collection key (the duplicate-material check at\n`src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:94-97` deliberately compares\nraw `byte[]` with `MessageDigest.isEqual` instead). The finding is that this code is a loaded\ngun: the class is `Serializable`, is a `SecretKey`, and behaves in a way that will destroy or blow\nup the first key anyone outside these two methods compares.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nMake the comparison non-destructive for foreign material and total for destroyed keys. Zero only\nbuffers this object owns, and treat a missing key as \"not equal\" rather than an exception:\n\n```java\n        @Override\n        public boolean equals(Object other) {\n            if (this == other) {\n                return true;\n            }\n            if (!(other instanceof SecretKey)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n            if (bytes == null) {\n                return false;\n            }\n            byte[] mine = null;\n            try {\n                mine = getEncoded();\n                byte[] theirs = ((SecretKey) other).getEncoded();\n                return theirs != null\n                        && theirs.length == AES_KEY_BYTES\n                        && MessageDigest.isEqual(mine, theirs);\n            } catch (RuntimeException unavailable) {\n                // A key that cannot expose its bytes is simply not equal to this one.\n                return false;\n            } finally {\n                if (mine != null) {\n                    Arrays.fill(mine, (byte) 0);\n                }\n            }\n        }\n```\n\nThe `finally` block erases only `mine`, which this object cloned for itself. `theirs` is left\nuntouched — its owner decides when that key dies. If the goal was to scrub every transient copy of\na secret, note that this method cannot honour that goal for another class's array, and the\ncomparison itself leaves an unerasable copy inside `MessageDigest.isEqual`'s parameters; that is\nanother reason to prefer not offering value equality on a key class at all.\n\n### SEC-03: `hashCode()` derives a 32-bit fingerprint from live AES key bytes\n\n- **Severity:** Low\n- **CWE:** CWE-200: Exposure of Sensitive Information to an Unauthorized Actor\n- **Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/security/crypto/AesGcmDataProtector.java:440-445`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n        @Override\n        public synchronized int hashCode() {\n            if (bytes == null) {\n                return 0;\n            }\n            return Arrays.hashCode(bytes);\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\nThe class goes out of its way not to disclose key material: `toString()` returns\n`\"DestroyableAesKey[redacted]\"` (line 449) and serialization is refused outright. `hashCode()`\nreopens that hole. `Arrays.hashCode(bytes)` is a cheap, deterministic, fully reversible-of-candidate\nfunction of the secret key, so the 32-bit value is a key fingerprint available to anything that\nreads it — and `hashCode()` is read implicitly and constantly: `HashMap`/`HashSet`/`Hashtable`\nbucketing, `String.format`, assertion and error text built from a map, debuggers, profilers, and\n`Map.toString()`/`List.toString()` renders, which pull in the *keys* of a collection and therefore\nits hash buckets in some renderers. Once that value reaches a log line or a crash report, an\nattacker holding a candidate key set (default or derived keys, a leaked provisioning template, a\nkey with meaningful entropy loss) can confirm a candidate with a single comparison, without any\ncrypto at all. That is a practical offline verification oracle for exactly the class of weak or\nshared key material a KMS is supposed to rule out.\n\nThe method also breaks the contract of the erasure design it sits inside. `hashCode()` is\nvalue-based while `destroy()` nulls the bytes, so an instance inserted into a hash collection\nbefore `close()` reports a different hash afterwards and can never be found or removed again —\na `HashMap` entry that keeps a reference to the key object alive past the erasure the whole class\nexists to guarantee. JDK key classes such as `SecretKeySpec` behave the same way for identity\nreasons, which is the correct signal: a secret key should not have value-based hashing.\n\nAs with SEC-02 the exposure is latent — `DestroyableAesKey` is private and never escapes into a\ncollection in this tree — so this is hardening against a future caller rather than a live leak.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nDo not derive a value from key bytes. Either fall back to identity semantics, or reject hashing\noutright so a caller cannot unknowingly put a key in a hash collection or print it:\n\n```java\n        @Override\n        public int hashCode() {\n            // Never derive a value from key bytes: the 32-bit digest of a secret key is a\n            // verifiable fingerprint and it goes into logs, assertions and collection\n            // internals. Identity semantics only.\n            return System.identityHashCode(this);\n        }\n```\n\nIf value equality from SEC-02 is kept, identity hashing makes the pair inconsistent, which is the\nright outcome for a secret-bearing class — in that case remove both overrides and document that\n`DestroyableAesKey` must never be used as a map key or compared by value, since a secret key\nobject has no legitimate reason to need either.\n\n## 6. Verified Controls and Checks With No Finding\n\nThe following were examined and are working as intended. Each is stated with the behaviour that\nshows it, so the absence of a finding is auditable rather than assumed.\n\n- **AEAD primitive and tag strength.** `AES/GCM/NoPadding` with `GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, nonce)`\n  and `TAG_BITS = 128` (lines 34, 36-38, 168-170). No unauthenticated mode anywhere: no ECB, CBC\n  without HMAC, CTR, DES/3DES, RC4, or MD5/SHA-1-based MAC. `grep` for weak-algorithm identifiers\n  returned nothing.\n- **Nonce generation.** A fresh 12-byte nonce is generated per `encrypt()` call from a\n  `SecureRandom` created in the constructor (lines 107, 162-163) and carried in the header for\n  decryption. No nonce reuse by construction, no counter persisted in the clear, no static or\n  derived nonce — the *budget* around this is SEC-01; the generation itself is correct. No\n  `Math.random()`, `java.util.Random`, `ThreadLocalRandom`, or time-seeded seeding exists in the\n  source.\n- **AAD framing and domain separation.** `addAuthenticatedData` binds\n  `len(purpose) ‖ purpose ‖ header ‖ len(context) ‖ context` (lines 300-305). The explicit 1-byte\n  length prefixes make the framing injective, so a `purpose`/`context` pair cannot be re-split to\n  reproduce the same AAD byte string, and the header (magic, version, algorithm, key-ID length, key\n  ID, nonce) is covered, which binds the envelope's own routing data to the tag. `purpose` is fixed\n  per instance and capped at 64 bytes; `context` is validated per call and capped at 64 KiB.\n- **No decryption oracle.** Every rejection path — bad length, bad magic, wrong version, wrong\n  algorithm byte, bad key-ID length, unsafe key-ID characters, unknown key ID, tag mismatch,\n  over-long plaintext, closed instance, exhausted budget — throws the same\n  `DataProtectionException` with a single fixed message (lines 197-203, 209-221, 262-279, 308-312).\n  Unknown key IDs additionally run a real decryption against a random per-instance `unknownKey`\n  (lines 108-109, 215-219) so a missing key is not answerable faster than a wrong key, removing the\n  key-ID enumeration timing/enumeration signal.\n- **Exception design without information leakage.** `DataProtectionException` passes\n  `(GENERIC_MESSAGE, null, false, false)` — no cause chain, no suppression, no stack trace — so\n  neither message nor trace can reveal which check failed or any internal state. The tests assert\n  the generic message and `getCause() == null`.\n- **Input validation at the trust boundary.** `SAFE_IDENTIFIER = [A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._/-]{0,63}`\n  plus a US-ASCII byte-length re-check (lines 47, 327-331) is applied to every key ID both at\n  construction and again on every parse (line 278), so a hostile envelope cannot smuggle control\n  characters, over-long IDs, or non-ASCII encodings into the key-ring lookup or into anything an\n  integrator might log. `Byte.toUnsignedInt` is used for the length field, and\n  `input.remaining() < keyIdLength + NONCE_BYTES + TAG_BYTES` is checked before any slice — with a\n  whole-envelope length ceiling applied *before* parsing (lines 197-201) — so `parseEnvelope` has\n  no path to an unchecked `ByteBufferUnderflowException` or an allocation driven by attacker data.\n- **Resource-exhaustion bounds.** Plaintext capped at 16 MiB\n  (`MAX_ALLOWED_PLAINTEXT_BYTES`, validated before encryption and re-checked on the decrypted\n  length at line 220), context at 64 KiB, key ring at 32 entries, key ID and purpose at 64 bytes\n  each. Decrypt's upper length bound is derived from `maxPlaintextBytes`, so a 10-byte header\n  claiming a huge plaintext cannot force a large allocation.\n- **Key material handling and lifetime.** The constructor clones every supplied key\n  (`new KeyEntry(key.clone())`, line 99) so caller-side mutation cannot alter the ring, rejects\n  non-256-bit material and duplicate IDs, and rejects duplicate *material* across IDs via the\n  constant-time `MessageDigest.isEqual` compare (lines 94-97). Copies are destroyed if the\n  constructor fails partway (lines 110-114). Per-operation keys are `DestroyableAesKey` instances\n  that are `destroy()`-ed in a `finally` after each cipher operation, and `sealed`/`ParsedEnvelope`\n  buffers are zeroed in `finally` blocks (lines 171-180, 361-377). `close()` destroys every ring\n  key, zeroes `unknownKey` and `purpose`, and sets `closed` under the write lock (lines 242-256).\n- **Fail-closed lifecycle.** `encrypt`, `decrypt`, and `close` take the read/write halves of one\n  `ReentrantReadWriteLock` and call `ensureOpen()` before touching key state (lines 152-155,\n  205-208, 242-251, 308-312), so a closed instance cannot seal or open data and `close()` cannot\n  interleave with an in-flight operation. The ring map itself is `Collections.unmodifiableMap`.\n- **Serialization posture.** `DestroyableAesKey.writeObject` throws\n  `NotSerializableException(\"Secret keys cannot be serialized\")` (lines 452-454), so a key cannot\n  be written out even if an enclosing object is made serializable. Reviewed against\n  `exploiting-insecure-deserialization`: the codebase contains no `ObjectInputStream`, no\n  `readObject`/`readResolve`, no Jackson/Gson/Fastjson/XStream deserialization, no YAML loading,\n  no JNDI lookup, and no reflection on attacker-controlled class names. The fixed `serialVersionUID`\n  on `DataProtectionException` is not a risk here because nothing deserializes it and the class\n  carries no fields. The serialization markers that do exist are the defensive `writeObject` block,\n  not a gadget surface.\n- **No injection, command, or filesystem sinks.** No `Runtime.exec`, `ProcessBuilder`, shell\n  invocation, file/socket/HTTP I/O, SQL/LDAP/XPath/XML parsing, template or expression evaluation,\n  or `File`/`Path` construction from input exists in the reviewed sources; `purpose`/key IDs are\n  confined to AAD bytes and map lookups.\n- **No secret material in code, and no sensitive telemetry.** No key literals, base64 blobs,\n  passwords, or API tokens appear anywhere; keys arrive only from the caller-supplied ring, which\n  `README.md` correctly attributes to a secret manager/KMS. There is no `Logger`, `System.out`,\n  `System.err`, `printStackTrace`, or env-var read in the source, so there is nothing that could\n  log plaintext, key bytes, or AAD — the `toString()` redaction at line 449 is consistent with that\n  (the gap is SEC-03's `hashCode`).\n- **Platform-only cryptography.** The implementation uses only JCA/JCE\n  (`Cipher`, `GCMParameterSpec`, `SecretKey`, `MessageDigest.isEqual`, `SecureRandom`). No\n  hand-rolled primitives, no hand-rolled MAC or padding, no self-made tag comparison — and the two\n  places where secrecy-sensitive bytes *are* compared (`magic`, duplicate key material) both use\n  `MessageDigest.isEqual`, which is the constant-time comparison the situation calls for. The\n  AAD/key-ring checks that are not secret-dependent (version, algorithm byte) use ordinary\n  comparisons, which is appropriate.\n- **Behaviour reviewed against general OWASP/CWE knowledge, where no library skill applied.**\n  Two areas had no matching specialised skill in the library and were therefore checked against\n  general practice rather than a loaded skill: (1) crypto object lifecycle and zeroisation\n  discipline (`DestroyableAesKey`, `ParsedEnvelope.destroy()`, `sealed` zeroing, the constructor's\n  failure-path cleanup) — reviewed as CWE-664 resource-lifetime reasoning; this produced SEC-02 and\n  informed SEC-03, and the copy/erase coverage in `encrypt()`/`decrypt()`/`close()` is otherwise\n  complete.
